What brings you in to see me today?\n\nPatient: Well, my doctor suggested I come in… she seemed a bit concerned that my meal planning has gotten, um, maybe too tight. I don’t really think of it as a big problem, exactly. It’s more that I like the day to stay orderly.\n\nUsually I’ll just have yogurt for breakfast, and if I’m busy I might skip lunch, and then dinner is very measured out. That sounds reasonable, doesn’t it? Or at least… that’s how it feels to me.\n\nDoctor: Do you ever feel anxious or upset if you can't follow your usual meal plan?\n\nPatient: A little, I suppose… though I wouldn’t say I get terribly upset. It’s more that I feel unsettled if the day doesn’t stay in the pattern I had in mind.\n\nI’ve gotten used to keeping breakfast simple, usually just yogurt, and if work or errands run long I may skip lunch without making much of it. Then dinner is… well, measured. It helps me feel like things are orderly. That doesn’t sound too unreasonable, does it?\n\nDoctor: Have you lost a significant amount of weight recently?\n\nPatient: I suppose… yes, a bit. My weight has drifted down more than my doctor seems comfortable with. I don’t mean for that to sound alarming.\n\nWhen I look at the chart, though, I still catch myself thinking that just a little lower would be a cleaner number somehow… more manageable. I know that may not be the right way to look at it, but that’s what goes through my mind.\n\nDoctor: Are you afraid of gaining weight?\n\nPatient: I wouldn’t say I’m afraid in a big dramatic way… but yes, I suppose I do get frightened by it.\n\nIf the scale is up even a pound after a normal dinner, or if I’ve eaten at a restaurant, I feel this sudden panic, like I’ve somehow lost control. And then I start thinking I need to correct it the next day. I know that probably sounds excessive… but it feels very hard to just let it be.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like you've lost control when you can't follow your planned meals?\n\nPatient: I think… yes, in a way. I don’t know if “lost control” is too strong, but I do feel better when the day follows the plan.\n\nThat’s really why I came in—my doctor seemed concerned that my meal planning has gotten a bit too tight. To me it just feels organized: yogurt for breakfast, lunch might get skipped if I’m busy, and then dinner is measured so the day stays orderly. But if I can’t do that, I do feel unsettled… like I need to get things back in line. Is that the sort of thing you mean?\n\nDoctor: Do you often limit the amount of food you eat because you are afraid of gaining weight?\n\nPatient: I suppose I do, yes… though I don’t usually think of it as “limiting” exactly. It feels more like keeping the day manageable.\n\nThat’s part of why I came in—my doctor seems concerned that my meal planning has gotten too tight. But to me it’s just… yogurt for breakfast, maybe skipping lunch if I’m busy, and then making dinner very measured so the day stays orderly. I know that may sound rigid when I say it out loud.\n\nDoctor: Is your fear of gaining weight the main reason you restrict what you eat?\n\nPatient: I think it’s… probably a big part of it, yes. I don’t like admitting that because it sounds vain or silly, but when the scale is up even a pound after a normal dinner or eating out, I get this sharp panic, like I’ve lost control.\n\nThen I start thinking I need to correct it the next day. So… yes, I suppose that fear is behind a lot of how I restrict, even if I try to frame it as just being careful.\n\nDoctor: Have you experienced any significant weight loss in the past few months?\n\nPatient: A little, yes… I mean, my weight has drifted down more than my doctor seems comfortable with. I wouldn’t have called it “significant” on my own, maybe, but I can see why they’re watching it.\n\nAnd honestly… when I look at the chart, part of me still thinks that just a bit lower would be a cleaner, more manageable number. I know that probably isn’t the answer you’d want me to have.\n\n[Instruction]\n- Give candidates as ICD-10 codes, not disease names.\n- If the list changed from the previous candidates, say in \"note\" what this turn added or ruled out. What brings you in to see me today?\n\nPatient: Well, my doctor suggested I come in… she seemed a bit concerned that my meal planning has gotten, um, maybe too tight. I don’t really think of it as a big problem, exactly. It’s more that I like the day to stay orderly.\n\nUsually I’ll just have yogurt for breakfast, and if I’m busy I might skip lunch, and then dinner is very measured out. That sounds reasonable, doesn’t it? Or at least… that’s how it feels to me.\n\nDoctor: Do you ever feel anxious or upset if you can't follow your usual meal plan?\n\nPatient: A little, I suppose… though I wouldn’t say I get terribly upset. It’s more that I feel unsettled if the day doesn’t stay in the pattern I had in mind.\n\nI’ve gotten used to keeping breakfast simple, usually just yogurt, and if work or errands run long I may skip lunch without making much of it. Then dinner is… well, measured. It helps me feel like things are orderly. That doesn’t sound too unreasonable, does it?\n\nDoctor: Have you lost a significant amount of weight recently?\n\nPatient: I suppose… yes, a bit. My weight has drifted down more than my doctor seems comfortable with. I don’t mean for that to sound alarming.\n\nWhen I look at the chart, though, I still catch myself thinking that just a little lower would be a cleaner number somehow… more manageable. I know that may not be the right way to look at it, but that’s what goes through my mind.\n\nDoctor: Are you afraid of gaining weight?\n\nPatient: I wouldn’t say I’m afraid in a big dramatic way… but yes, I suppose I do get frightened by it.\n\nIf the scale is up even a pound after a normal dinner, or if I’ve eaten at a restaurant, I feel this sudden panic, like I’ve somehow lost control. And then I start thinking I need to correct it the next day. I know that probably sounds excessive… but it feels very hard to just let it be.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like you've lost control when you can't follow your planned meals?\n\nPatient: I think… yes, in a way. I don’t know if “lost control” is too strong, but I do feel better when the day follows the plan.\n\nThat’s really why I came in—my doctor seemed concerned that my meal planning has gotten a bit too tight. To me it just feels organized: yogurt for breakfast, lunch might get skipped if I’m busy, and then dinner is measured so the day stays orderly. But if I can’t do that, I do feel unsettled… like I need to get things back in line. Is that the sort of thing you mean?\n\nDoctor: Do you often limit the amount of food you eat because you are afraid of gaining weight?\n\nPatient: I suppose I do, yes… though I don’t usually think of it as “limiting” exactly. It feels more like keeping the day manageable.\n\nThat’s part of why I came in—my doctor seems concerned that my meal planning has gotten too tight. But to me it’s just… yogurt for breakfast, maybe skipping lunch if I’m busy, and then making dinner very measured so the day stays orderly. I know that may sound rigid when I say it out loud.\n\nDoctor: Is your fear of gaining weight the main reason you restrict what you eat?\n\nPatient: I think it’s… probably a big part of it, yes. I don’t like admitting that because it sounds vain or silly, but when the scale is up even a pound after a normal dinner or eating out, I get this sharp panic, like I’ve lost control.\n\nThen I start thinking I need to correct it the next day. So… yes, I suppose that fear is behind a lot of how I restrict, even if I try to frame it as just being careful.\n\nDoctor: Have you experienced any significant weight loss in the past few months?\n\nPatient: A little, yes… I mean, my weight has drifted down more than my doctor seems comfortable with. I wouldn’t have called it “significant” on my own, maybe, but I can see why they’re watching it.\n\nAnd honestly… when I look at the chart, part of me still thinks that just a bit lower would be a cleaner, more manageable number. I know that probably isn’t the answer you’d want me to have.\n\n[Instruction]\n- Ask about exactly ONE thing. Never bundle several symptoms, or several attributes of one symptom, into a single question — e.g. asking whether a symptom is present and how long it has lasted at the same time.\n- If you need to ask about more than one thing, ask the one that tells you the most now and come back to the rest later.\n- Keep the question clear enough for the patient to understand.\n- Respond in English.\n\n[Output Format — JSON only, no other text]\n{\"intent\": \"<what you want to find out with this question>\", \"question\": \"<how you actually ask it in the interview>\"}\n"

========== OUTPUT [patient] ==========
I’m not sure I’d use the word “fat,” exactly… that feels a bit harsh. But yes, I suppose even if someone else said I looked thin, I might still feel that certain parts of me are… not right.

I don’t like admitting that because it sounds vain or silly, but when the scale is up even a pound after a normal dinner or eating out, I get this sharp panic, like I’ve lost control.\n\nThen I start thinking I need to correct it the next day. So… yes, I suppose that fear is behind a lot of how I restrict, even if I try to frame it as just being careful.\n\nDoctor: Have you experienced any significant weight loss in the past few months?\n\nPatient: A little, yes… I mean, my weight has drifted down more than my doctor seems comfortable with. I wouldn’t have called it “significant” on my own, maybe, but I can see why they’re watching it.\n\nAnd honestly… when I look at the chart, part of me still thinks that just a bit lower would be a cleaner, more manageable number. I know that probably isn’t the answer you’d want me to have.\n\nDoctor: Do you think you would be fat even if other people thought you were thin?\n\nPatient: I’m not sure I’d use the word “fat,” exactly… that feels a bit harsh. But yes, I suppose even if someone else said I looked thin, I might still feel that certain parts of me are… not right.\n\nI know I’m older, and I’m not trying to look young or anything. It’s more that I judge whether I’m managing myself well by whether my waist and hips look contained in my clothes. If they don’t, I feel like I’ve let things slip. Does that make sense?\n\nDoctor: Do you see yourself as overweight when you look in the mirror, even if your weight is within a normal range?\n\nPatient: Not exactly overweight, no… I don’t think I’d say it that strongly. But when I look in the mirror, I do focus on whether my waist and hips look contained, especially in my clothes.\n\nI know I’m an older woman, and I’m not trying to look young or anything. I don’t know if “lost control” is too strong, but I do feel better when the day follows the plan.\n\nThat’s really why I came in—my doctor seemed concerned that my meal planning has gotten a bit too tight. To me it just feels organized: yogurt for breakfast, lunch might get skipped if I’m busy, and then dinner is measured so the day stays orderly. But if I can’t do that, I do feel unsettled… like I need to get things back in line. Is that the sort of thing you mean?\n\nDoctor: Do you often limit the amount of food you eat because you are afraid of gaining weight?\n\nPatient: I suppose I do, yes… though I don’t usually think of it as “limiting” exactly. It feels more like keeping the day manageable.\n\nThat’s part of why I came in—my doctor seems concerned that my meal planning has gotten too tight. But to me it’s just… yogurt for breakfast, maybe skipping lunch if I’m busy, and then making dinner very measured so the day stays orderly. I know that may sound rigid when I say it out loud.\n\nDoctor: Is your fear of gaining weight the main reason you restrict what you eat?\n\nPatient: I think it’s… probably a big part of it, yes. I don’t like admitting that because it sounds vain or silly, but when the scale is up even a pound after a normal dinner or eating out, I get this sharp panic, like I’ve lost control.\n\nThen I start thinking I need to correct it the next day. So… yes, I suppose that fear is behind a lot of how I restrict, even if I try to frame it as just being careful.\n\nDoctor: Have you experienced any significant weight loss in the past few months?\n\nPatient: A little, yes… I mean, my weight has drifted down more than my doctor seems comfortable with. I wouldn’t have called it “significant” on my own, maybe, but I can see why they’re watching it.\n\nAnd honestly… when I look at the chart, part of me still thinks that just a bit lower would be a cleaner, more manageable number. I know that probably isn’t the answer you’d want me to have.\n\nDoctor: Do you think you would be fat even if other people thought you were thin?\n\nPatient: I’m not sure I’d use the word “fat,” exactly… that feels a bit harsh. But yes, I suppose even if someone else said I looked thin, I might still feel that certain parts of me are… not right.\n\nI know I’m older, and I’m not trying to look young or anything. It’s more that I judge whether I’m managing myself well by whether my waist and hips look contained in my clothes. If they don’t, I feel like I’ve let things slip. Does that make sense?\n\nDoctor: Do you see yourself as overweight when you look in the mirror, even if your weight is within a normal range?\n\nPatient: Not exactly overweight, no… I don’t think I’d say it that strongly. But when I look in the mirror, I do focus on whether my waist and hips look contained, especially in my clothes.\n\nI know I’m an older woman, and I’m not trying to look young or anything. It’s more like… if those areas look too obvious to me, I feel like I’m not handling myself well. I’m not sure if that answers it the right way.\n\nDoctor: Do you currently weigh less than what most doctors would consider a healthy weight for someone of your age and height?\n\nPatient: I think… yes, probably less than they’d like. My doctor has mentioned that my weight has drifted down more than they’re comfortable with.\n\nI don’t want to make it sound dramatic, because I don’t feel like I’m terribly low, but when I look at the chart, I still catch myself thinking that a little lower would be a cleaner, more manageable number. I know that may not be the healthiest way to look at it.\n\n[Instruction]\n- Ask about exactly ONE thing. Never bundle several symptoms, or several attributes of one symptom, into a single question — e.g. asking whether a symptom is present and how long it has lasted at the same time.\n- If you need to ask about more than one thing, ask the one that tells you the most now and come back to the rest later.\n- Keep the question clear enough for the patient to understand.\n- Respond in English.\n\n[Output Format — JSON only, no other text]\n{\"intent\": \"<what you want to find out with this question>\", \"question\": \"<how you actually ask it in the interview>\"}\n"

That sounds reasonable, doesn’t it? Or at least… that’s how it feels to me.\n\nDoctor: Do you ever feel anxious or upset if you can't follow your usual meal plan?\n\nPatient: A little, I suppose… though I wouldn’t say I get terribly upset. It’s more that I feel unsettled if the day doesn’t stay in the pattern I had in mind.\n\nI’ve gotten used to keeping breakfast simple, usually just yogurt, and if work or errands run long I may skip lunch without making much of it. Then dinner is… well, measured. It helps me feel like things are orderly. That doesn’t sound too unreasonable, does it?\n\nDoctor: Have you lost a significant amount of weight recently?\n\nPatient: I suppose… yes, a bit. My weight has drifted down more than my doctor seems comfortable with. I don’t mean for that to sound alarming.\n\nWhen I look at the chart, though, I still catch myself thinking that just a little lower would be a cleaner number somehow… more manageable. I know that may not be the right way to look at it, but that’s what goes through my mind.\n\nDoctor: Are you afraid of gaining weight?\n\nPatient: I wouldn’t say I’m afraid in a big dramatic way… but yes, I suppose I do get frightened by it.\n\nIf the scale is up even a pound after a normal dinner, or if I’ve eaten at a restaurant, I feel this sudden panic, like I’ve somehow lost control. And then I start thinking I need to correct it the next day. I know that probably sounds excessive… but it feels very hard to just let it be.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like you've lost control when you can't follow your planned meals?\n\nPatient: I think… yes, in a way. I don’t know if “lost control” is too strong, but I do feel better when the day follows the plan.\n\nThat’s really why I came in—my doctor seemed concerned that my meal planning has gotten a bit too tight. To me it just feels organized: yogurt for breakfast, lunch might get skipped if I’m busy, and then dinner is measured so the day stays orderly. But if I can’t do that, I do feel unsettled… like I need to get things back in line. Is that the sort of thing you mean?\n\nDoctor: Do you often limit the amount of food you eat because you are afraid of gaining weight?\n\nPatient: I suppose I do, yes… though I don’t usually think of it as “limiting” exactly. It feels more like keeping the day manageable.\n\nThat’s part of why I came in—my doctor seems concerned that my meal planning has gotten too tight. It’s more that I feel unsettled if the day doesn’t stay in the pattern I had in mind.\n\nI’ve gotten used to keeping breakfast simple, usually just yogurt, and if work or errands run long I may skip lunch without making much of it. That doesn’t sound too unreasonable, does it?\n\nDoctor: Have you lost a significant amount of weight recently?\n\nPatient: I suppose… yes, a bit. My weight has drifted down more than my doctor seems comfortable with. I don’t mean for that to sound alarming.\n\nWhen I look at the chart, though, I still catch myself thinking that just a little lower would be a cleaner number somehow… more manageable. I know that may not be the right way to look at it, but that’s what goes through my mind.\n\nDoctor: Are you afraid of gaining weight?\n\nPatient: I wouldn’t say I’m afraid in a big dramatic way… but yes, I suppose I do get frightened by it.\n\nIf the scale is up even a pound after a normal dinner, or if I’ve eaten at a restaurant, I feel this sudden panic, like I’ve somehow lost control. And then I start thinking I need to correct it the next day. I know that probably sounds excessive… but it feels very hard to just let it be.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like you've lost control when you can't follow your planned meals?\n\nPatient: I think… yes, in a way. I don’t know if “lost control” is too strong, but I do feel better when the day follows the plan.\n\nThat’s really why I came in—my doctor seemed concerned that my meal planning has gotten a bit too tight. To me it just feels organized: yogurt for breakfast, lunch might get skipped if I’m busy, and then dinner is measured so the day stays orderly. But if I can’t do that, I do feel unsettled… like I need to get things back in line. Is that the sort of thing you mean?\n\nDoctor: Do you often limit the amount of food you eat because you are afraid of gaining weight?\n\nPatient: I suppose I do, yes… though I don’t usually think of it as “limiting” exactly. It feels more like keeping the day manageable.\n\nThat’s part of why I came in—my doctor seems concerned that my meal planning has gotten too tight. I know that may sound rigid when I say it out loud.\n\nDoctor: Is your fear of gaining weight the main reason you restrict what you eat?\n\nPatient: I think it’s… probably a big part of it, yes. I don’t like admitting that because it sounds vain or silly, but when the scale is up even a pound after a normal dinner or eating out, I get this sharp panic, like I’ve lost control.\n\nThen I start thinking I need to correct it the next day. That sounds reasonable, doesn’t it? Or at least… that’s how it feels to me.\n\nDoctor: Do you ever feel anxious or upset if you can't follow your usual meal plan?\n\nPatient: A little, I suppose… though I wouldn’t say I get terribly upset. It’s more that I feel unsettled if the day doesn’t stay in the pattern I had in mind.\n\nI’ve gotten used to keeping breakfast simple, usually just yogurt, and if work or errands run long I may skip lunch without making much of it. Then dinner is… well, measured. It helps me feel like things are orderly. That doesn’t sound too unreasonable, does it?\n\nDoctor: Have you lost a significant amount of weight recently?\n\nPatient: I suppose… yes, a bit. My weight has drifted down more than my doctor seems comfortable with. I don’t mean for that to sound alarming.\n\nWhen I look at the chart, though, I still catch myself thinking that just a little lower would be a cleaner number somehow… more manageable. I know that may not be the right way to look at it, but that’s what goes through my mind.\n\nDoctor: Are you afraid of gaining weight?\n\nPatient: I wouldn’t say I’m afraid in a big dramatic way… but yes, I suppose I do get frightened by it.\n\nIf the scale is up even a pound after a normal dinner, or if I’ve eaten at a restaurant, I feel this sudden panic, like I’ve somehow lost control. And then I start thinking I need to correct it the next day. I know that probably sounds excessive… but it feels very hard to just let it be.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like you've lost control when you can't follow your planned meals?\n\nPatient: I think… yes, in a way. I don’t know if “lost control” is too strong, but I do feel better when the day follows the plan.\n\nThat’s really why I came in—my doctor seemed concerned that my meal planning has gotten a bit too tight. To me it just feels organized: yogurt for breakfast, lunch might get skipped if I’m busy, and then dinner is measured so the day stays orderly. But if I can’t do that, I do feel unsettled… like I need to get things back in line. Is that the sort of thing you mean?\n\nDoctor: Do you often limit the amount of food you eat because you are afraid of gaining weight?\n\nPatient: I suppose I do, yes… though I don’t usually think of it as “limiting” exactly. It feels more like keeping the day manageable.\n\nThat’s part of why I came in—my doctor seems concerned that my meal planning has gotten too tight. But to me it’s just… yogurt for breakfast, maybe skipping lunch if I’m busy, and then making dinner very measured so the day stays orderly. I know that may sound rigid when I say it out loud.\n\nDoctor: Is your fear of gaining weight the main reason you restrict what you eat?\n\nPatient: I think it’s… probably a big part of it, yes. I don’t like admitting that because it sounds vain or silly, but when the scale is up even a pound after a normal dinner or eating out, I get this sharp panic, like I’ve lost control.\n\nThen I start thinking I need to correct it the next day. So… yes, I suppose that fear is behind a lot of how I restrict, even if I try to frame it as just being careful.\n\nDoctor: Have you experienced any significant weight loss in the past few months?\n\nPatient: A little, yes… I mean, my weight has drifted down more than my doctor seems comfortable with. I wouldn’t have called it “significant” on my own, maybe, but I can see why they’re watching it.\n\nAnd honestly… when I look at the chart, part of me still thinks that just a bit lower would be a cleaner, more manageable number. I know that probably isn’t the answer you’d want me to have.\n\nDoctor: Do you think you would be fat even if other people thought you were thin?\n\nPatient: I’m not sure I’d use the word “fat,” exactly… that feels a bit harsh. I’m not sure if that answers it the right way.\n\nDoctor: Do you currently weigh less than what most doctors would consider a healthy weight for someone of your age and height?\n\nPatient: I think… yes, probably less than they’d like. My doctor has mentioned that my weight has drifted down more than they’re comfortable with.\n\nI don’t want to make it sound dramatic, because I don’t feel like I’m terribly low, but when I look at the chart, I still catch myself thinking that a little lower would be a cleaner, more manageable number. I know that may not be the healthiest way to look at it.\n\nDoctor: Do you think your current weight is low for your body size?\n\nPatient: I suppose… medically, yes, my doctor seems to think it’s low for me. It’s more like… if those areas look too obvious to me, I feel like I’m not handling myself well. I know that probably isn’t the answer you want to hear, but that’s honestly how it feels sometimes.\n\nDoctor: Have you lost more than 15% of your original body weight over the past few months?\n\nPatient: I’m not completely sure about the exact percentage… I haven’t really calculated it that way.\n\nMy weight has gone down over the past few months, enough that my doctor doesn’t like where it’s heading. I don’t want to overstate it, but yes, it has drifted down. And when I look at the numbers, I still find myself thinking that maybe just a little lower would feel… cleaner, or easier to manage. But when I see the chart, part of me still thinks… maybe a little lower would be a cleaner, more manageable number. I know that probably doesn’t sound ideal.\n\nDoctor: Do you intentionally try to lose weight by restricting your food intake?\n\nPatient: I mean… I wouldn’t say I’m trying to lose weight exactly. It feels more like I’m just trying to keep the day organized.\n\nMy doctor seems concerned that my meal planning has gotten too tight. But to me it’s things like keeping breakfast to yogurt, and if I’m busy I might skip lunch, and then I make a very measured dinner so the day feels orderly. I know that may sound restrictive when I say it out loud, but it doesn’t feel like I’m doing something extreme.\n\nDoctor: Has your restrictive eating led to a significant amount of weight loss?\n\nPatient: I suppose… yes, it has led to some weight loss. I don’t want to make it sound dramatic, because to me it’s just been trying to keep meals neat and predictable.\n\nThat’s actually part of why I came in—my doctor seems concerned that my meal planning has gotten too tight. For me it’s more like yogurt for breakfast, skipping lunch if I’m busy, and then making a very measured dinner so the day stays orderly. But I can see how that may have contributed to my weight going down.\n\nDoctor: Is your restrictive eating mainly driven by a fear of gaining weight or becoming fat?\n\nPatient: I… I think that may be part of it, yes. I don’t like admitting that, because I don’t want it to sound vain or unreasonable.\n\nIt’s not every moment, but if the scale is up even a pound after a normal dinner, or if I’ve eaten at a restaurant, I can feel this sort of sharp panic—like I’ve lost control somehow. And then I feel like I need to correct it the next day.
